syndrome locations, which are the two endpoints of its support.

Proof.

We will first prove (1) and (3) together with a weaker version of (2) where we show that
\mathcal{T}_{n}
acts linearly on a subset of level-
n
syndrome locations corresponding to the two endpoints.

To prove this, we will need the following fact. Suppose that during the operation of some non-elementary gadget
G
, a particular vertex
v
is always an endpoint of any constituent elementary gadget appearing during the operation of
G
whose support includes
v
. In this case, we claim that
v
is a linear vertex. To show this, we define the notion of control vertices of an elementary gadget, which are vertices whose syndromes influence the feedback operator that the elementary gadget applies. It is clear that the control vertices for the
\mathcal{T}_{0}
and
\mathcal{M}_{0}
gadgets are the middle vertex and the middle two vertices, respectively. The presence or lack of syndromes at the endpoints of these gadgets does not affect what feedback operator the elementary gadget applies (so that these vertices are linear vertices of
\mathcal{T}_{0}
and
\mathcal{M}_{0}
, respectively). Then, for any such vertex
v
, the total feedback operator applied by
G
is independent of the syndrome at this vertex. Denoting the vertex by
v
,

G[\sigma,\varepsilon]=G[\tau|_{v}]\oplus G[\tau|_{v}\oplus\sigma,\varepsilon],

(4.2)

thus proving linearity for this vertex.

Next, we provide a proof by induction that
EC_{n}
,
\mathcal{M}_{n}
,
\mathcal{T}_{n}
, and
\mathcal{I}_{n}
act linearly on their endpoints; in particular, that the endpoints satisfy the above criterion. We have already seen that this is true for the endpoints of the level-
0
gadgets. To prove the induction step, we assume the statement is true for level-
n
gadgets and prove it for level-
n+1
gadgets. To do so, we write the level-
n+1
gadgets in terms of the level-
n
gadgets. We note that by inspecting the recursive substitution rule, the endpoints of level-
n+1
gadgets are always endpoints of level-
n
gadgets. Therefore, if the endpoints of the level-
n
gadgets are always non-control vertices for elementary gadgets, the same must hold for the level-
n+1
gadgets, completing the proof.

We finally will prove that the midpoint level-
n
vertices of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
are also linear vertices; this case needs to be analyzed separately because some of the
\mathcal{T}_{n-1}
gadgets appearing in
\mathcal{T}_{n}
have these vertices in their interior (in other words, these are their “control” vertices, i.e. the feedback of the gadgets can be conditioned on the syndromes in these vertices). We will first use the fact that, from its definition,
\mathcal{T}_{0}
is a reversible gate, and satisfies the property that
\mathcal{T}_{0}[\sigma_{1}\oplus\sigma_{2},\varepsilon]=\mathcal{T}_{0}[\sigma_{1},\varnothing]\oplus\mathcal{T}_{0}[\sigma_{2},\varnothing]\oplus\mathcal{T}_{0}[0,\varepsilon]
. Similarly, for a circuit
\mathcal{C}
of purely
\mathcal{T}_{0}
gates,
\mathcal{C}[\sigma_{1}\oplus\sigma_{2},\varepsilon]=\mathcal{C}[\sigma_{1},\varnothing]\oplus\mathcal{C}[\sigma_{2},\varnothing]\oplus\mathcal{C}[0,\varepsilon]
. Our induction hypothesis will be that

\mathcal{T}_{n}[\tau|_{\Sigma_{n}}\oplus\sigma,\varepsilon]=\mathcal{T}_{n}[\tau|_{\Sigma_{n}}]\oplus\mathcal{T}_{n}[\sigma,\varepsilon],

and we will prove an analogous property at level
n+1
. We have already shown the base case when
n=0
. Using the substitution rule for the modified Tsirelson’s automaton, we may write
\mathcal{T}_{n+1}
in terms of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
with layers of
EC_{n}
inserted in between. By the induction hypothesis and due to the fact that
\mathcal{I}_{n}
has linear vertices at its endpoints, for each layer of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
and
\mathcal{I}_{n}
gates (denote one such layer by
[\mathcal{I}_{n},\cdots,\mathcal{T}_{n}]
), we can write

[\mathcal{I}_{n},\cdots,\mathcal{T}_{n}][\tau|_{\Sigma_{n}}\oplus\sigma,\varepsilon_{1}]=[\mathcal{I}_{n},\cdots,\mathcal{T}_{n}][\tau|_{\Sigma_{n}}]\oplus[\mathcal{I}_{n},\cdots,\mathcal{T}_{n}][\sigma,\varepsilon_{1}],

(4.3)

where
\varepsilon_{1}
denotes restriction of noise to that particular time step, and where the first term importantly has support only on the level-
n
vertices (due to the coarse-graining action of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
and
\mathcal{I}_{n}
). During the layers of
EC_{n}
, linearity holds at the level-
n
vertices, so an identical statement to the above equation can be made about such layers.

Applying layer by layer and repeating this identity, we find that
\mathcal{T}_{n+1}[\tau|_{\Sigma_{n}}\oplus\sigma,\varepsilon_{1}]=\mathcal{T}_{n+1}[\tau|_{\Sigma_{n}}]\oplus\mathcal{T}_{n+1}[\sigma,\varepsilon_{1}]
, and further restricting
\tau|_{\Sigma_{n}}
to
\tau|_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
proves the induction step.
15
15

15

In fact, this proves an even stronger property for the
\mathcal{T}_{n}
gadgets, in particular, that all points in
\Sigma_{n-1}
are linear.

∎

In addition to linearity, another important idea that we will utilize in the fault tolerance proof is the idea of gadget nilpotence, which essentially quantifies how good gadgets are at performing error correction:

Definition 4.4

(Gate nilpotence)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{0}
be a
K
-qubit gate. A gate gadget
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
is said to be nilpotent if for
\varepsilon=\text{syn}(\mathcal{O})
where
\mathcal{O}
is a Pauli operator supported within a single level-0 gadget located in
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
, and for
\sigma=\sigma|_{\Sigma_{\ell}}
, we have

(EC_{\ell}^{\otimes K}\circ\mathcal{G}_{\ell})[\sigma,\varepsilon]=\mathcal{G}_{\ell}[\sigma],

where
EC_{\ell}^{\otimes K}
are assumed to be noise-free. A gadget
\mathcal{G}
is said to be nilpotent at level
\ell
if
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
is nilpotent.

More informally, if a gadget is nilpotent at level
\ell
, a single level-0 gadget failure within a level-
\ell
version of the gadget (assuming the input syndrome configuration is coarse-grained at level
\ell
) is eliminated, either during the operation of the gadget or by the subsequent noiseless error correction gadgets afterward. Note that the nilpotence level
\ell
refers to the number of iterations of the
inner
simulation needed for the desired error suppression.

To prove fault tolerance, we will need to establish the nilpotence (ideally at as low a level as possible) of the distinct gates that make up the error correction gadgets. The following lemma establishes this:

Lemma 4.2
.

The following gadgets are nilpotent: (a)
\mathcal{I}
is nilpotent at level 1, (b)
\mathcal{T}
is nilpotent at level 1, (c)
\mathcal{I}
is nilpotent at level 2, (d)
\mathcal{T}
is nilpotent at level 2, and (e)
\mathcal{M}
is nilpotent at level 2.

Proof.

We will be using the terms “clean input” or “damage-free input” to denote a configuration of syndromes with no nontrivial syndromes.

First, we show the following fact. If a gate
\mathcal{G}
(where
\mathcal{G}_{0}
is a
K
-bit gate) is nilpotent at level
\ell
and
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
is a part of a different gadget
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{i}
with
i>\ell
, then a single level-0 gadget failure located within the support of
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
in an otherwise noise-free gadget
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{i}
cannot change the output of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{i}
. To prove this, we can write
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{i}
in terms of level-
\ell

EC
and gate gadgets. Since the
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
where the failure occurs is preceded by a noise-free
EC_{\ell}^{\otimes K}
gadget, then the input syndromes to this
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
must be coarse-grained at level
\ell
. Next, because
i>\ell
,
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
must be followed by
EC_{\ell}^{\otimes K}
which is noise-free by assumption that there is only one fault in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{i}
. Thus, by nilpotence, we can replace
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}[\sigma,\varepsilon]\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{\ell}[\sigma]
and the gadget failure will not affect the output of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{i}
.

Due to the linearity of endpoints of gadgets, the proof for statements (a-d) does not depend on the syndromes at the linear points. Calling
\sigma
the input syndromes and
\sigma_{\ell}
the input syndromes restricted to linear points, we can evolve the gadget
\mathcal{G}
assuming the input
\sigma\oplus\sigma_{\ell}
, and
\mathbb{F}_{2}
add the gadget action
\mathcal{G}[\sigma_{\ell}]
at the end.

(a) Consider
EC_{1}\circ\mathcal{I}_{1}[\sigma,\varepsilon]
. The only gate failures in the support of
\mathcal{I}_{1}
that are possible are those of
\mathcal{I}_{0}
gadgets; therefore, the output damage is limited to at most one bit (i.e. whose syndromes are contained in a 0 cell). This is cleaned up by the noiseless
EC_{1}
at the end. Together with the linearity of
\mathcal{I}_{1}
on the level-1 coarse-grained syndromes, this gives (a).

(b) Next, consider
EC_{1}^{\otimes 2}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1}[\sigma,\varepsilon]
. Similarly, the possible failures in the support of
\mathcal{T}_{1}
are limited to either
\mathcal{I}_{0}
or
\mathcal{T}_{0}
. In the former case, one finds that the output damage is limited to one bit; in the latter, the output damage has support on two bits, with each bit belonging to a different 1-cell. As a result, the noise-free
EC_{1}^{\otimes 2}
at the end restores the correct coarse-grained configuration.

(c) We can write

EC_{2}\circ\mathcal{I}_{2}=EC_{2}\circ(EC_{1}^{\otimes 3}\circ\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\otimes 3})^{5}\circ EC_{1}^{\otimes 3}

(4.4)

One possible level-0 gadget failure occurs within the support of an
\mathcal{I}_{1}
; from (a) and the fact from beginning of this proof, such failures are inconsequential. Another possible level 0 failure is within
EC_{1}
(excluding the last layer of
EC_{1}
), which will produce damage whose support is at most on a single 1-cell; the support of the damage is left unchanged by
\mathcal{I}_{1}
s and is coarse-grained to level 1 by the subsequent noise-free
EC_{1}
s. Finally, it is eliminated by the noise-free
EC_{2}
at the end. If the level 0 failure occurs within the last
EC_{1}
, the damage due to this failure will also be supported in at most a single 1-cell and will be erased by the subsequent noise-free
EC_{2}
.

(d) We can write

EC_{2}^{\otimes 2}\circ\mathcal{T}_{2}=EC_{2}^{\otimes 2}\circ(EC_{1}^{\otimes 6}\circ[\mathcal{I}_{1},...\mathcal{T}_{1}])^{5}\circ EC_{1}^{\otimes 6}

(4.5)

where
(EC_{1}^{\otimes 6}\circ[\mathcal{I}_{1},...\mathcal{T}_{1}])^{5}
stands for layers where
I_{1}
and
T_{1}
gadgets are applied in some combination that we do not specify. If the failure occurs in the support of
\mathcal{I}_{1}
or
\mathcal{T}_{1}
, by properties (a) and (b) and the fact from the beginning of the proof, the damage is cleaned by the subsequent layer of
EC_{1}^{\otimes 6}
. If one of the gadgets within the support of
EC_{1}
fails, the output damage will still have support within a 1 cell after the subsequent
\mathcal{I}_{1}
or
\mathcal{T}_{1}
gadgets
16
16

16

To see this, note that by direct verification, a single gadget failure in
EC_{1}
will lead to damage with support on a 1-cell. A single layer of clean
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{1}
following this will at most transport the damage to a different 1-cell.
. The damage will finally be coarse-grained to level 1 by the subsequent
EC_{1}
, and will remain coarse-grained at level 1 and fitting within a 1-cell until it is eliminated by the final noise-free
EC_{2}
. Similarly to part (c), if the level 0 failure occurs within the last
EC_{1}
, the damage due to this failure will be erased by the subsequent noise-free
EC_{2}
.

(e) We can write

EC_{2}^{\otimes 3}\circ\mathcal{M}_{2}=EC_{2}^{\otimes 3}\circ(EC_{1}^{\otimes 9}\circ[\mathcal{I}_{1},...\mathcal{T}_{1}])^{5}\circ EC_{1}^{\otimes 9}\circ\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\otimes 3}\circ(EC_{1}^{\otimes 9}\circ[\mathcal{I}_{1},...\mathcal{T}_{1}])^{5}\circ EC_{1}^{\otimes 9}

(4.6)

where, as before,
(EC_{1}^{\otimes 9}\circ[\mathcal{I}_{1},...\mathcal{T}_{1}])^{5}
denotes (now different) layers of level-1 identity and swap gates. We are not able to use the linearity property anymore to remove non-trivial input syndromes in
\Sigma_{2}
because these syndromes can be located where
\mathcal{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
acts non-linearly.

If a level-0 gadget failure occurs at any point after the layer of
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\otimes 3}
, then the damage right after any subsequent layer of
EC_{1}^{\otimes 9}
(or the noise-free layer of
EC_{2}
) will be coarse-grained at level 1 and will fit in a 1-cell. Then, same considerations as in previous parts apply, since the remaining gates to be applied consist entirely of
\mathcal{I}_{1}
,
\mathcal{T}_{1}
, and
EC_{1}
.

If the failure occurs at any point before the layer of
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\otimes 3}
, we note that the damage before the layer of
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\otimes 3}
will always fit in an
1
-cell. This is because the gadgets
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{1}
are nilpotent against level-0 failures within them, and failure of any of the
EC_{1}
gadgets produces damage contained within a single 1-cell (which we call
m=1
damage). We note that
m=1
input damage continues to remain
m=1
damage under any sequence of clean
\mathcal{I}_{1}
,
\mathcal{T}_{1}
, and
EC_{1}
(since the action of a single one of these gadgets does not grow the size of the damage beyond a 1-cell). Hence
this case – as well as the final remaining case where the level-0 failure occurs within the
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\otimes 3}
layer – reduces to studying the
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\otimes 3}
layer with either input damage fitting in a 1-cell or a clean input with a single level-0 gadget failure inside
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\otimes 3}
. By explicitly checking all possible scenarios of this, we find that in all of them, the output damage will have support at most the size of a single
\mathcal{M}_{1}
gadget (which is the size of a 2-cell). After the following layer of
EC_{1}
gadgets, the resulting damage will be coarse-grained to level 1 and will fit in a single 2-cell. Thus, we can reduce the level by 1, mapping the problem of considering
\mathcal{M}_{2}
with a level-2 input and a single failed
\mathcal{M}_{1}
in the middle layer to considering an
\mathcal{M}_{1}
gadget with a level-1 input and a single failed
\mathcal{M}_{0}
in the middle layer. Such a failure is cleaned up after running the rest of the gadget and following with a noise-free
EC
and we thus find that
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is nilpotent.
∎

We emphasize that while
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is nilpotent,
\mathcal{M}_{1}
is
not
; this is the reason why our proof of fault tolerance in this setting must go up by several levels. As an explicit example demonstrating this, consider
EC_{1}^{\otimes 3}\mathcal{M}_{1}
acting on the input state
111\,000\,000
,
with a fault occurring in the first
\mathcal{T}_{0}
gate to be applied (c.f. the circuit for
\mathcal{M}_{1}
given in (
3.11
)).
This produces the state
111\,001\,100
after the first layer of the circuit constituting
\mathcal{M}_{1}
. The action of the rest of
EC_{1}^{\otimes 3}\circ\mathcal{M}_{1}
on this configuration produces the output
111\,111\,000
, which contains a logical error (a similar problem does not afflict
\mathcal{M}_{2}
due to the nilpotence of
\mathcal{T}_{1}
).

We are now ready to prove the Gate and
EC
conditions for the present set of gadgets.

Proposition 4.3
.

Assume an inner and outer fault tolerant prescription where all elementary gadgets undergo
p
-bounded gadget error model (where each of the gadgets
\mathcal{I}_{0}
,
\mathcal{T}_{0}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{0}
can fail).
Then:

1.

The gate gadgets
\mathcal{I}_{k}
,
\mathcal{T}_{k}
and
\mathcal{M}_{k}
satisfy
(Gate
A
)_{k,m}
and
(Gate
B
)_{k,m}
properties and

2.

The
\widetilde{EC}=EC_{k}
gadget satisfies
(EC
A
)_{k,m}
and
(EC
B
)_{k,m}
properties

for
k\geq 3
and
m=1
(c.f. Def.
4.2
).

In the following, we will split the proof into separate parts, one for each property. In several places we will prove slightly stronger results with
k=2
.

Proof (
EC
B).

We will show
EC
B for
k=2
, and
m=1
.
The definition of the
EC
B property allows for either a single or no level-0 gadget failures to occur within the support of the
EC
gadget; in the former case, the input can be taken to be properly coarse-grained at level
k
.

Consider first the case where the failure occurs inside the
EC
gadget.
Since the input syndromes are supported on
\Sigma_{k}
and
EC_{k}
acts linearly on these locations, and since
EC_{k}[\sigma|_{\Sigma_{k}}]=\sigma|_{\Sigma_{k}}
, for the purposes of demonstrating
EC
B we can assume that we are given a clean codeword as input.
We can write
EC_{2}
as

EC_{2}=EC_{1}^{\otimes 3}\circ\mathcal{M}_{1}\circ EC_{1}^{\otimes 3}\circ\mathcal{T}_{1}\mathcal{I}_{1}\circ EC_{1}^{\otimes 3}\circ\mathcal{M}_{1}\circ EC_{1}^{\otimes 3}\circ\mathcal{I}_{1}\mathcal{T}_{1}\circ EC_{1}^{\otimes 3}\circ\mathcal{M}_{1}\circ EC_{1}^{\otimes 3}

(4.7)

If the level-0 failure occurs in the support of one of the
\mathcal{T}_{1}
or
\mathcal{I}_{1}
gate gadgets, it is inconsequential because of their nilpotence (Lemma
4.2
). If it is located inside any of the
EC_{1}
or
\mathcal{M}_{1}
gadgets, one can directly verify that the output damage of
EC_{2}
is contained within a single 1 cell (the fact that the input is coarse-grained at level 2 is necessary for this to be true). This damage will be cleaned up by an ideal level-2 decoder.

Now let us consider the case when
s=0
and
m=1
. The first layer of
EC_{1}^{\otimes 3}
inside of
EC_{2}
will coarse-grain this damage to level 1. Then, treating this as an input, the operation of the remainder of the gadget is equivalent to level reducing by 1, in which case the damage fits in a 0-cell and the remaining operation of the gadget is equivalent to
EC_{1}
which cleans up such an input. This is again consistent with a 1-cell filter acting on the output, which proves
EC
B for
k=2
,
m=1
.

Finally, let us show
(EC\text{ }B)_{3,1}
is true given that
(EC\text{ }B)_{2,1}
is true. First,
EC_{3}[\sigma,\varepsilon]
can be expressed through level 2 gates and
EC
gadgets. When
s=1
,
m=0
and the failure occurs inside one of the level 2 gates, it is inconsequential because of the nilpotence property (Lemma
4.2
). If the error occurs within any of the
EC_{2}
gadgets, we use the fact that
(EC\text{ }B)_{2,1}
holds. Therefore, the failure either has no effect or it causes
1
-cell damage at the output of the
EC_{2}
gadget. If this is the last
EC_{2}
in the circuit for
EC_{3}
, this proves the claim. Otherwise, the damage is input into the subsequent level-2 gate, wherein it is coarse-grained to level 1 by the first layer of clean
EC_{1}
in the level-2 gate. Therefore, we can formally reduce the level by 1 and consider this damage as level-0 input damage to a level-1 gate. If the gate is
\mathcal{I}
or
\mathcal{T}
, the output damage still fits in a 0-cell, which means (going back up one level) it is cleaned up by the subsequent
EC_{2}
. If the gate is
\mathcal{M}
, one can explicitly check that the damage is either cleaned up or remains unchanged, in which case it will be cleaned up by the subsequent
EC_{2}
. This concludes the proof of
(EC\text{ }B)_{3,1}
.
∎

Proof (Gate A).

We first will show a stronger property, namely
(\text{Gate A})_{2,1}
. We will assume the gate gadget acts on
K
bits. Consider first the case
s=1
, and
m_{i}=0
for
i=0,\cdots,K
. By Lemma
4.2
,
\mathcal{I}_{2}
,
\mathcal{T}_{2}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
are nilpotent, i.e.
EC_{2}\circ\mathcal{I}_{2}[\sigma,\varepsilon]=\mathcal{I}_{2}[\sigma]
,
EC_{2}^{\otimes 2}\circ\mathcal{T}_{2}[\sigma,\varepsilon]=\mathcal{T}_{2}[\sigma]
and
EC_{2}^{\otimes 3}\circ\mathcal{M}_{2}[\sigma,\varepsilon]=\mathcal{M}_{2}[\sigma]
. According to the same logic as in the proof of
EC
B, if
\varepsilon
occurs before the last layer of
EC_{1}
in either of the gate gadgets, then
\mathcal{T}_{2}[\sigma,\varepsilon]=\mathcal{T}_{2}[\sigma]
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}[\sigma,\varepsilon]=\mathcal{M}_{2}[\sigma]
. If
\varepsilon
occurs during the last layer of
EC_{1}
in either of the gate gadgets, then it causes damage confined to at most a single 1-cell, consistent with an 1-cell filter acting on the output.

We now consider the case
s=0
, and
m_{i}=1
for some
i
, i.e. when the input damage (relative to the nearest level-2 coarse-grained configuration) is contained within a single 1-cell. After the first layer of
EC_{1}
, the syndromes lie entirely in
\Sigma_{1}
. The operation of the remainder of both gadgets can be then understood by reducing the level by 1, i.e. by considering
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{1}
with input damage contained in a 0-cell. The output will have damage contained at most in a 0-cell per each 1-cell, which when going back up 1 level is consistent with
m=1
.

Finally,
(\text{Gate A})_{3,1}
property follows from
(\text{Gate A})_{2,1}
by similar considerations to those in the proof of
EC
B.
∎

Proof (Gate B).

Assume the gate gadget acts on
K
bits. For the case
s=1
, and
m_{i}=0
for
i=0,\cdots,K
, this condition follows from nilpotence of all gates at level 2 together with
(EC\text{ }B)_{2,1}
and considerations from the proof of
Gate
A. The output state is thus equivalent to having undergone a noiseless gate with possible
1
-cell damage at the end. The ideal decoder can eliminate this damage.
The case
s=0
, and
m_{i}=1
can be similarly analyzed.
∎

Proof (
EC
A).

First, we express
EC_{3}
through level-2 gadgets only, assuming a single level-0 gadget failure and now an
arbitrary
input state. The circuit starts with a layer of
EC_{2}^{\otimes 9}
, and we denote
t_{0}
to be the point in time right after this layer.

Consider first the situation when the level-0 gadget failure occurs after
t_{0}
. In that case, the first layer of
EC_{2}^{\otimes 3}
is error-free, and the state at
t_{0}
is coarse-grained at level 2. A level-0 gate failure then must occur in the support of one of the subsequent level-2 gates
\mathcal{G}_{2}
or inside one of the subsequent layers of
EC_{2}^{\otimes 3}
. From Lemma
4.2
, we know that in the former case, the state is not changed.
If the failure occurred in the support of one of the
EC_{2}^{\otimes 3}
layers, we combine considerations for
k=2
from the proofs of
(EC\text{ }B)_{2,1}
and
(\text{Gate }A)_{2,1}
to find that the damage is limited to the support of a 1-cell after each gate or
EC_{2}
layer. This is consistent with adding a 1-cell filter at the end.

Consider now the case when the level-0 gadget failure occurred before
t_{0}
(i.e. in the support of one of the
EC_{2}
gadgets). For reference, this gadget is written out in Eq. (
4.7
).
If the level-0 gadget failure occurred
within the first layer of
EC_{1}^{\otimes 9}
, the output of one of these
EC_{1}
gadgets contains arbitrary damage in its support and the damage is coarsed-grained to level 1 for the other
EC_{1}
gadgets. A brute-force computation (by a computer algebra software; see App.
C.1
) shows that the output of the subsequent
EC_{1}\circ\mathcal{M}_{1}
gadget has the property that there exists (a non-unique) input configuration which is coarse-grained to level 1 that would cause the same output. Let us refer to this property as producing a
reversible output
. Choosing a level-1 coarse-grained input with such an output, the action of the gadget with the level-0 failure can be replaced by the action of the gadget on this input, which will be coarse grained at level 3, fulfilling the
EC\text{ }A
condition.

Next, consider the case when the gadget failure occurs within the first layer of
EC_{2}^{\otimes 3}
, in particular after the first layer of
EC_{1}^{\otimes 9}
and before the last layer of
EC_{1}^{\otimes 9}
. Because the first layer of
EC_{1}
gadgets is noise-free, if the fault occurs either in the support of subsequent
\mathcal{I}_{1}
or
\mathcal{T}_{1}
, due to the nilpotence of those gates, it has no consequence. If it occurs elsewhere, the damage will fit in a 2-cell and after the last layer of
EC_{1}^{\otimes 9}
(which is noise-free) the damage is coarse-grained to level 1. Considering the entire
EC_{3}
gadget, we see that the input to the first
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is now coarse-grained at level 2 apart from one 2-cell where the input is coarse-grained at level 1. This reduces to the consideration in the previous paragraph upon reducing the level by one, i.e. we replace
\mathcal{M}_{2}
with
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
EC_{3}
with
EC_{2}
. The
\mathcal{M}_{1}
in question produces a reversible output, which is equivalent to a level-1 coarse-grained input to
EC_{2}
and a noise-free operation of
EC_{2}
. This damaged is clearly coarse-grained at level 2, which upon undoing level reduction, corresponds to being coarse-grained at level 3.

Finally, if the error occurred in the last layer of
EC_{1}^{\otimes 9}
(within the first layer of
EC_{2}^{\otimes 3}
), the output is unchanged under an
1
-cell filter. After the next gate followed by the next layer of
EC_{2}
the additional damage will be cleaned up, as per the
(\text{Gate }A)_{2,1}
and
(EC\text{ }B)_{2,1}
properties. Therefore,
(EC\text{ }A)_{k,m}
holds for
k=3
and
m=1
.

This proves the Proposition.

∎

Having established the EC and Gate properties, fault tolerance readily follows:

Corollary 4.1
.

Consider a classical circuit
\mathcal{C}
on 1 bit which consists of
T
idle operations. Construct a fault-tolerant circuit corresponding to
n
repetitions of the outer simulation
(\widetilde{FT})^{n}(\mathcal{C})
with inner simulation level
k=3
and
m=1
. Assuming a
p
-bounded gadget error model in
(\widetilde{FT})^{n}(\mathcal{C})
,
\mathcal{C}
experiences an error model that is a
(Ap)^{2^{n}}
-bounded wire error model for some constant
A
. The probability that the initial logical state
b(0)
will get corrupted in time
T
is

\mathbb{P}\left(\widetilde{D}_{n}(s(t=T))\neq\widetilde{D}_{n}(s(t=0))\right)\leq T\cdot(Ap)^{2^{n}}

(4.8)

Proof.

With
k=3
and
m=1
, both
EC
and Gate properties are satisfied by the previous Lemma. Consider a circuit
\mathcal{C}
and its fault tolerant simulation
\widetilde{FT}(\mathcal{C})
. Proposition
4.1
indicates that if the gadget error model in
\widetilde{FT}(\mathcal{C})
is
p
-bounded, adding a layer of ideal decoder and pulling it through the circuit, the gadget error model for
\mathcal{C}
is
Ap^{2}
-bounded. Starting from
(\widetilde{FT})^{n}(\mathcal{C})
and iterating this
n
times as done in the first proof of fault tolerance for the modified Tsirelson’s automaton gives a
(Ap)^{2^{n}}
-bounded gadget failure model. By a union bound,
\mathbb{P}\left(\widetilde{D}_{n}(s(t=T))\neq\widetilde{D}_{n}(s(t=0))\right)\leq T\cdot(Ap)^{2^{n}}
. Selecting
n=(\log_{3}L)/k=(\log_{3}L)/3
shows that the memory lifetime is exponential in a power of
L
.
∎

5
Fault tolerance of the toric code automaton

The full toric code automaton is constructed in Sec.
3
. It is a measurement and feedback circuit consisting of simultaneous action of a layer of
X
- and
Z
-type elementary gadgets which, according to Remark
6
, can be defined to operate under a general
p
-bounded gadget error model.
From the analysis in Appendix
A
, see Sec.
3.1
for a summary of the results, we can reduce this to studying a decoupled gadget error model. In this error model, the
X
-gadgets apply
X
-type feedback and noise operators only, and
Z
gadgets, similarly, apply
Z
-type feedback and noise. Fact
1
shows that under this error model the operation of the full toric code automaton can be reduced to considering separate
X
and
Z
automata. At the end of this section in Corollary
5.4
, we finally explain how fault tolerance of the full toric code automaton follows from that of the
X
and
Z
automata separately.

Therefore, most of this section will be devoted to showing the fault tolerance of the
X
part of the toric code automaton,
which is comprised of
X
-type gadgets that apply Pauli
X
feedback only and experience Pauli
X
noise. To construct this automaton, we use the inner and outer fault tolerant simulation prescription. We then develop a modification of the extended rectangles method, prove that it can be used to derive the appropriate error suppression as a function of the number of fault tolerant simulations, and use this show the fault tolerance of the
X
-type automaton.

The rest of this section is structured as follows. The main difficulty in proving the fault tolerance of
X
-type automaton is that the supports of neighboring gadgets must overlap, which can cause interactions between the operations of the gadgets and enable errors to spread (this is not an issue for standard circuit concatenation). In Subsec.
5.1
, we explore a property we call syndrome confinement, which allows us to show that the interaction between any pair of neighboring gadgets in our construction is limited to the boundary between them, and moreover, this region of interaction cannot influence error syndromes in the rest of space. This property is needed to adapt the extended rectangles method to the setting of the toric code automaton. In addition, the ideal decoder has to operate in a global way for the toric code (due to the global nature of the pushforward map), while the ideal decoder for Tsirelson’s automaton is local. To deal with these challenges, we introduce the notion of spatial truncation and appropriately adapt the concepts of
\ast
-decoders and the Gate and EC conditions. This is done in Subsec.
5.2
.

Having developed a modified exRecs formalism for the toric code, we show that fault tolerance follows from Gate and EC conditions, and finally, in Subsec.
5.3
, we show that these conditions hold for the gadgets comprising the toric code automaton. To show that these properties hold, we use the strategy partially developed in the previous section via the measurement-and-feedback version of Tsirelson’s automaton. Namely, we introduce the notion of nilpotence for the toric code gadgets and provide a computer-aided proof of this property. We show that there exists a level
k
for the inner simulation such that the associated outer simulation achieves error suppression. Upon a single iteration of the outer simulation, the original circuit is simulated with
\widetilde{p}
-bounded noise, where
\widetilde{p}\sim(Ap)^{2}
. Repeated outer simulation thus achieves (quasi)exponential suppression of the logical error rate with the system size.
Finally, we also discuss fault-tolerant initialization and readout in Subsec.
5.4
, and discuss some preliminary numerical results in Subsec.
5.5
.

5.1
Confinement of syndromes

For the reasons explained above, we will focus on
X
-type gadgets and drop the
X
label for the remainder of the text unless otherwise specified. To deal with gadgets that act on overlapping domains, we introduce a property that we call confinement. In our proof, we interchangeably work with physical operators (which are supported on qubit locations, i.e. the links of the lattice) and error syndromes (located on the vertices of the lattice). This is because some of the arguments are easier to formulate using operators, while others are easier to formulate using syndromes.

It is more natural to think of confinement in the syndrome language. Intuitively speaking, the input syndromes and the syndromes created due to noise in a confinement region cannot affect the syndromes outside of it. If the overlap of the supports between two neighboring gadgets is a confinement region, then the gadgets can “interact” in this region, but what happens in this region cannot influence syndromes elsewhere.
Such a property will limit how neighboring gadgets can affect each other’s operation and thus will play an important role in proving fault tolerance.
The gadgets that we introduced for the toric code automaton were designed so that any two neighboring gadgets overlap on confinement regions only.

Definition 5.1

(Confinement)
.

Consider a layer of gadgets
\mathcal{L}(G)
of some level
n
within the toric code automaton. A subset
\Lambda\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
of syndrome locations is called a confinement region if for input syndrome
\sigma\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{|\mathcal{L}_{0}|}
and Pauli noise realization with syndrome
\varepsilon
, we have

\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]=\eta\big|_{\Lambda}\oplus\mathcal{L}(G;\mathcal{L}_{0}\setminus\Lambda)[\sigma,\varepsilon]

(5.1)

where
\eta\big|_{\Lambda}=\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]\big|_{\Lambda}
. Recall that the notation
\mathcal{L}(G;\mathcal{L}_{0}\setminus\Lambda)
is defined in Def.
3.22
.
Syndromes in
\Lambda
are said to be confined.

If
\Lambda
is a confinement region for a layer
\mathcal{L}(G)
of the toric code automaton, we will often simply say that
\Lambda
is a confinement region for
G
.

The “non-communication” property between the neighboring gadgets that overlap on confinement regions will be made more precise below. Although the confinement region can be arbitrarily shaped, for simplicity of presentation, we restrict ourselves to the case (relevant for the toric code automaton) where any two level-
n
neighboring gadgets overlap on
n
-links, which are confinement regions for the associated layer of gadgets.

Remark 3
.

From this point on, we assume that the gadgets of the toric code automaton (i.e. the gate
\mathcal{G}_{n}
and
EC_{n}
gadgets) have support on connected regions and that neighboring gadgets can overlap on a union of
n
-links or on points in
\Sigma_{n}
. We also assume that that overlap between any two gadgets is a confinement region for the associated layer of gadgets and the corners of any gadgets are linear vertices; this is proved in Prop.
5.1
for the toric code automaton.

Fact 3

(Confinement separates the bulk from the boundary)
.

Consider a layer of level-
n
gates
\mathcal{L}(G)
as well as a certain level-
n
gadget
G_{1}
in this layer with vertex support
\Lambda_{1}
and boundary
\partial\Lambda_{1}
. Assume that overlap between
G_{1}
and any other gadget
G_{i}
in the same layer overlaps on a set of boundary
n
-links that we denote as
\lambda_{1i}
, i.e.
\mathrm{supp}_{v}(G_{1})\cap\mathrm{supp}_{v}(G_{i})=\lambda_{1i}
. We also assume that the boundary of the gadget
G_{1}
, denoted
\partial\Lambda_{1}=\cup_{i}\lambda_{1i}
, is a confinement region. Then, we have

\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]=\eta|_{\partial\Lambda_{1}}\oplus(G_{1};\Lambda_{1}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{1})[\sigma,\varepsilon]\oplus\mathcal{L}(G;\mathcal{L}_{0}\setminus\Lambda_{1})[\sigma,\varepsilon].

(5.2)

where
\eta|_{\partial\Lambda_{1}}=\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]|_{\partial\Lambda_{1}}
.

Proof.

Because
\partial\Lambda_{1}
is a confinement region, we can write by definition

\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]=\eta\big|_{\partial\Lambda_{1}}\oplus\mathcal{L}(G;\mathcal{L}_{0}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{1})[\sigma,\varepsilon]

(5.3)

Because of the assumption that all gadgets overlap on boundaries only, for any gadget
G_{i}\neq G_{1}
, we can write
\mathrm{supp}_{v}(G_{1})\cap\mathrm{supp}_{v}(G_{i})\cap(\mathcal{L}_{0}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{1})=\varnothing
. Thus, the operation of
\mathcal{L}(G;\mathcal{L}_{0}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{1})
factors as such:

\mathcal{L}(G;\mathcal{L}_{0}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{1})[\sigma,\varepsilon]=(G_{1};\Lambda_{1}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{1})[\sigma,\varepsilon]\oplus\mathcal{L}(G;\mathcal{L}_{0}\setminus\Lambda_{1})[\sigma,\varepsilon].

(5.4)

This proves the fact.
∎

Fact 4

(Confinement implies non-communication)
.

Consider a layer of level-
n
gadgets
\mathcal{L}(G)
and any two neighboring level-
n
gadgets
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
in this layer, which have vertex supports
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
respectively. The pair of gadgets overlap on boundary
n
-links only and each such
n
-link is a confinement region with respect to
\mathcal{L}(G)
. Call
\partial\Lambda_{1}
and
\partial\Lambda_{2}
the set of boundary vertices of the two gadgets respectively. Then,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]=\eta|_{\partial\Lambda_{1}\cup\partial\Lambda_{2}}\oplus(G_{1};\Lambda_{1}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{1})[\sigma,\varepsilon]\oplus(G_{2};\Lambda_{2}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{2})[\sigma,\varepsilon]\oplus\mu\big|_{\mathcal{L}_{0}\setminus(\Lambda_{1}\cup\Lambda_{2})}

(5.5)

where
\eta
and
\mu
are some syndrome vectors that generally depend on the input syndromes, noise, and the operation of the layer of gadgets. The supports are illustrated below:

Proof.

Using the previous fact with respect to the gadget
G_{1}
, we can write

\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]=\eta^{\prime}|_{\partial\Lambda_{1}}\oplus(G_{1};\Lambda_{1}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{1})[\sigma,\varepsilon]\oplus\mathcal{L}(G;\mathcal{L}_{0}\setminus\Lambda_{1})[\sigma,\varepsilon].

(5.6)

where
\eta^{\prime}|_{\partial\Lambda_{1}}=\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]|_{\partial\Lambda_{1}}
. Therefore,

\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]|_{\Lambda_{1}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{1}}=(G_{1};\Lambda_{1}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{1})[\sigma,\varepsilon].

(5.7)

Analogously, for the second gadget, using the same logic gives

\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]|_{\Lambda_{2}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{2}}=(G_{2};\Lambda_{2}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{2})[\sigma,\varepsilon].

(5.8)

Finally, we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]|_{\partial\Lambda_{1}\cup\partial\Lambda_{2}}\oplus\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]|_{\Lambda_{1}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{1}}\oplus\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]|_{\Lambda_{2}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{2}}\oplus\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]|_{\mathcal{L}_{0}\setminus(\Lambda_{1}\cup\Lambda_{2})}

\displaystyle=\eta|_{\partial\Lambda_{1}\cup\partial\Lambda_{2}}\oplus(G_{1};\Lambda_{1}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{1})[\sigma,\varepsilon]\oplus(G_{2};\Lambda_{2}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{2})[\sigma,\varepsilon]\oplus\eta|_{\mathcal{L}_{0}\setminus(\Lambda_{1}\cup\Lambda_{2})}

where in the first equality, we simply decomposed the output of a layer of gadgets over non-overlapping regions. In the second equality, we used Eqs. (
5.7
) and (
5.8
) and defined
\eta|_{\partial\Lambda_{1}\cup\partial\Lambda_{2}}=\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]|_{\partial\Lambda_{1}\cup\partial\Lambda_{2}}
and
\eta|_{\mathcal{L}_{0}\setminus(\Lambda_{1}\cup\Lambda_{2})}=\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]|_{\mathcal{L}_{0}\setminus(\Lambda_{1}\cup\Lambda_{2})}
. This completes the proof of the fact.
∎

As stated above, boundary segments of every gadget in the toric code automaton are confinement regions. We will now prove this fact:

Proposition 5.1
.

Consider the toric code gadgets obtained from inner simulation described in Subsec.
3.5.2
, and recall the definition of gadget linearity in Def.
4.3
. The following statements are true:

1.

Each
n
-link
\lambda
in the support of
EC_{n}
is a confinement region with respect to a layer
\mathcal{L}(EC_{n})
.

2.

The
EC_{n}
gadget is linear on all
n
-frame vertices
\Sigma_{n}
in its support.

3.

Each
n
-link (or union of links) highlighted in blue in the picture below is a (separate) confinement region for layers of
\mathcal{I}_{n}
,
\mathcal{T}_{n}^{h}
and
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{h}
gadgets, respectively:

where the links that are shown in gray are the
(n-1)
links of the lattice. Note that confinement regions corresponding to the top and bottom links
\mathcal{T}_{n}^{h}
and
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{h}
are a union of two or three horizontal
n
-links. For
\mathcal{T}_{n}^{v}
and
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{v}
, the confinement regions are found similarly by a
90^{\circ}
rotation.

4.

\mathcal{I}_{n}
,
\mathcal{T}_{n}^{h}
and
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{h}
act linearly on the
n
-frame vertices shown below:

where the links that are shown in gray are the
(n-1)
links of the lattice. For
\mathcal{T}_{n}^{v}
and
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{v}
, the linear points are obtained by a
90^{\circ}
rotation.

Proof.

We first will prove the claim regarding linearity. In the proof of Prop.
4.2
, we first proved that if during the operation of some non-elementary gadget a particular vertex is always an endpoint of any constituent elementary gadget, then such a vertex is a linear vertex. This fact is also true for the toric code for the same reason as given in Prop.
4.2
, except that endpoints of gadgets become their corners.

First, we show that the corner vertices of the level-
n
gadgets
\mathcal{I}_{n}
,
\mathcal{T}_{n}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{n}
are linear vertices. We show the aforementioned fact using induction on
n
. When
n=0
, this is true by definition of elementary gadgets. We then assume that this is true for
n
. We write
\mathcal{I}_{n+1}
,
\mathcal{T}_{n+1}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}
in terms of
\mathcal{I}_{n}
,
\mathcal{T}_{n}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{n}
and note that corner vertices of
\mathcal{I}_{n+1}
,
\mathcal{T}_{n+1}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}
are always corner vertices of
\mathcal{I}_{n}
,
\mathcal{T}_{n}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{n}
, thus proving the induction step.

Finally, we prove that for
\mathcal{T}_{n}
, the two remaining level-
n
vertices are linear vertices. We follow the proof in Prop.
4.2
. However, now one must write
\mathcal{T}_{n+1}
in terms of a 2D circuit of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
and
\mathcal{I}_{n}
interspersed with layers of
EC_{n}
; noting that the corner vertices of
EC_{n}
are linear vertices, the proof follows otherwise identically to that in Prop.
4.2
.

Next, we prove the claim about confinement regions. We first show the following fact. Consider a subset of vertices
\lambda
during the operation of a layer of gadgets
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
. For an elementary gadget
\mathcal{G}_{0}
, define the feedback support
\text{supp}_{f}(\mathcal{G}_{0})
to be the three vertices in the bottom border of
\mathcal{T}_{0}^{h}
, the four vertices in the bottom border of
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{h}
and the two vertices in the bottom border of
\mathcal{I}_{0}
as shown in the figure in Def.
3.20
. For vertically oriented gadgets, the feedback support is rotated accordingly. The use of the term ‘feedback support’ refers to the set of sites where syndromes can be modified due to the action of the gadget. We claim that
\lambda
is a confinement region with respect to a layer of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
if any elementary level-0 gadget
\mathcal{G}_{0}
during the operation of a layer of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
gadgets with
\text{supp}_{f}(\mathcal{G}_{0})\not\subseteq\lambda
has the property that
\text{supp}_{f}(\mathcal{G}_{0})\cap\lambda
is a subset of corners of
\mathcal{G}_{0}
. In other words, this means that the feedback support of
\mathcal{G}_{0}
is either contained in
\lambda
or overlaps with it on linear vertices of
\mathcal{G}_{0}
. Assuming this, calling
\overline{\lambda}
the complement of
\lambda
, we have
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{0})[\sigma,\varepsilon]|_{\overline{\lambda}}=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{0})[\sigma|_{\overline{\lambda}},\varepsilon|_{\overline{\lambda}}]|_{\overline{\lambda}}
. Applying the same logic layer by layer, after the entire operation of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
, we get

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{n})[\sigma,\varepsilon]=(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{n});\overline{\lambda})[\sigma,\varepsilon]\oplus\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{n})[\sigma,\varepsilon]|_{\lambda},

(5.9)

and setting
\eta|_{\lambda}=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{n})[\sigma,\varepsilon]|_{\lambda}
, this proves that
\lambda
is a confinement region.

Next, we will show that the above property is true for the boundary
n
-links of each of the gadgets, thereby proving the proposition. This can be shown by induction. First, consider
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{1})
for
\mathcal{G}_{1}=\mathcal{I}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{1}
. By direct inspection, the boundary 1-links of these gadgets have the property that any elementary gate that applies feedback at this boundary is either
\mathcal{M}_{0}
and
\mathcal{T}_{0}
whose feedback is contained entirely in a 1-link; otherwise, it is a level-0 gadget whose corners are contained in the 1-link. This also holds for
EC_{1}
, thereby proving the base case. Then, suppose the fact is true for
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
where
\mathcal{G}_{n}=\mathcal{I}_{n},\mathcal{T}_{n},\mathcal{M}_{n}
. We may write
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{n+1})
in terms of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
interspersed with layers of
\mathcal{L}(EC_{n})
. The boundaries of the level-
n+1
gadgets are always boundaries of the constituent level-
n
gadgets, and therefore the fact must be true for the level-
n+1
gadgets. This proves the induction step, and thus the claim regarding confinement regions.

∎

We now have all the tools needed to complete the proof of Lemma
3.1
, which states that the first half of a noiseless
EC_{n}
gadget coarse-grains input syndromes to
\Sigma_{n}
, and that
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{h/v},\mathcal{T}_{n}^{h/v}
act on coarse-grained states in a way analogous to the action of
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{h/v},\mathcal{T}_{0}^{h/v}
. We complete this proof below, and additionally show the statement of the Lemma in the case when the gadgets tile the entire lattice.

Proof.

(
Proof of Lemma
3.1
)

First, we will show the base case for
n=1
except now for the case when the lattice (or some connected subregion) is tiled with level-1 gadgets rather than for a single gadget restricted to its support. Suppose we tile the lattice with
EC_{1}
gadgets. Then, the set of 1-links are confinement regions for
EC_{1}
(as well as
R_{0}^{v}
and
R_{0}^{h}
) and therefore, one can evolve the syndromes in the bulk of these gadgets independently of syndromes on the boundaries of these gadgets and assume an arbitrary syndrome configuration on the boundary. After the first
R_{0}^{v}
there will be no syndromes remaining in the bulk as per the simpler argument (for part of the base case of Lemma
3.1
) from Sec.
3
. The remaining syndromes will be confined to the boundaries,
and the next round of
R_{0}^{v}
will coarse-grain these syndromes to level-1 vertices for the vertical boundaries, since the action of
R_{0}^{v}
restricted to the boundary is the same as that of Tsirelson’s 1D error correction gadget. The remaining syndromes on the horizontal boundaries will be coarse-grained at level 1 by the next two rounds of
R_{0}^{h}
. The base case for the gadgets
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{1}
is much simpler: these gadgets are either stacked horizontally or vertically and correspond to stacks of gadgets that apply feedback (similar to that of Tsirelson’s gadgets) on 1D lines each, which will send a level-1 syndrome input to a level-1 syndrome output corresponding to the action of a stack of appropriate level-0 gadgets.

Next, we show the induction step.
We can write
EC_{n}
in terms of a sequence of gate gadgets
\mathcal{G}_{n-1}
and a layer of
EC_{n-1}
gadgets covering the lattice between each gate gadget. By the induction step, after the first layer of
EC_{n-1}
, the syndromes are coarse-grained to level-
(n-1)
. Thus, we can study the action of the rest of the gadget by “reducing the level” by
n-1
. By the induction hypothesis,
\mathcal{G}_{n-1}
on such a coarse-grained input will keep the syndromes coarse-grained at level
n-1
and the action will correspond to the action of
\mathcal{G}_{0}
. Thus, level reduction allows us to formally view
(n-1)
-level frame as an effective level-0 lattice, where each gate
\mathcal{G}_{n-1}
is replaced with a corresponding level-0 gate, and each
EC_{n-1}
gadget has no effect, and thus can be ignored. Upon performing level reduction on the entire
EC_{n}
, we obtain an effective circuit for
EC_{1}
which coarse-grains the syndromes by the analysis in the base case.

Finally, we must prove that
\mathcal{M}^{h/v}_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}^{h/v}_{n}
will have the correct operation on coarse-grained syndrome configurations assuming the induction hypothesis. Once again, we write
\mathcal{M}^{h/v}_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}^{h/v}_{n}
in terms of
\mathcal{M}^{h/v}_{n-1}
,
\mathcal{T}^{h/v}_{n-1}
, and
EC_{n-1}
. Since the nontrivial syndromes are coarse-grained to live in
\Sigma_{n}
, we can perform an analogous form of level reduction by
n-1
levels to show that this analysis is equivalent to that of
\mathcal{M}^{h/v}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}^{h/v}_{1}
, which is dealt with by the base case.
∎

Finally, we prove the following fact for the toric code automaton which will be very useful later on.

Fact 5
.

Consider a covering of the lattice with level-
n
gadgets corresponding to a particular step of the toric code automaton. Call two neighboring gadgets
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
and
\Lambda_{12}
the vertices they overlap on. Call the supports of the two gadgets
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
. Then,

\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]|_{\Lambda_{12}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{12}}=(G_{1}\otimes G_{2};(\mathcal{R}_{1}\cup\mathcal{R}_{2})\setminus\partial(\mathcal{R}_{1}\cup\mathcal{R}_{2}))[\sigma,\varepsilon]|_{\Lambda_{12}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{12}}

(5.10)

Proof.

We first note that each boundary of a level-
n
gadget is a separate confinement region for level-
n
gadgets by Prop.
5.1
. Furthermore, if
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
are the union of all boundary segments of gadgets
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, then
\Lambda_{1}\cup\Lambda_{2}
is a confinement region. This follows directly from the arguments in Prop.
5.1
. In addition, calling
\mathcal{R}_{1}\cup\mathcal{R}_{2}\equiv\mathcal{R}
, we can write the outer boundary for the pair of gadgets as
(\partial\mathcal{R}_{1}\cup\partial\mathcal{R}_{2})\setminus(\partial\mathcal{R}_{1}\cap\partial\mathcal{R}_{2})\equiv\partial\mathcal{R}
. It is a confinement region as it is a union of boundary segments of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
.

Applying Fact
4
, with this choice of confinement region, we find

\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]=\eta\oplus(G_{1}\otimes G_{2};(\mathcal{R}_{1}\cup\mathcal{R}_{2})\setminus\partial\mathcal{R})[\sigma,\varepsilon]\oplus\mathcal{L}(G;\mathcal{L}_{0}\setminus(\mathcal{R}_{1}\cup\mathcal{R}_{2}\setminus\partial R))[\sigma,\varepsilon],

(5.11)

where
\eta=\mathcal{L}(G)[\sigma,\varepsilon]|_{\partial\mathcal{R}}
. Restricting the output to
\Lambda_{12}\setminus\partial\Lambda_{12}\subset\mathcal{R}\setminus\partial\mathcal{R}
proves the fact.
∎

This implies that the syndromes on a boundary between two gadgets (minus the endpoints of the boundary) can be reconstructed only by knowing the syndromes in the bulks of both of the gadgets.

5.2
Modification of the exRecs method

5.2.1
Main definitions

Now, we will define the technology needed to extend the exRecs method to the (
X
-part of the) toric code automaton. We will assume a fixed level
k
for inner simulation throughout. Let us first establish some definitions that we will use to count the number of faults due to noise:

Definition 5.2

(Failure in the support of a gadget)
.

We say that a level-0 gadget failed in the support of a
EC_{n}
or
\mathcal{G}_{n}
gadget if a failure occurred on this level-0 (elementary) gadget and the gadget’s support lies entirely within the support of the
EC_{n}
or
\mathcal{G}_{n}
gadget.

Definition 5.3

(exRec)
.

Assuming some fixed level
k
for inner simulation, an exRec for a gate
\mathcal{G}_{k}
consists of a spacetime region corresponding to the operation
\widetilde{EC}^{\otimes K}\circ\mathcal{G}_{k}\circ\widetilde{EC}^{\otimes K}
where
K
is the number of
k
-cells in the spatial support of
\mathcal{G}_{k}
. We say that such an exRec is centered at gadget
\mathcal{G}_{k}
. We call
\mathrm{supp}_{q}(R)
the spatial qubit support of an exRec
R
.

Definition 5.4

(Good/bad exRecs)
.

An exRec is good if the number of level-0 gadget failures in its spacetime support is
\leq 1
. Otherwise, it is bad.

We will utilize exRecs to prove the error suppression property for the fault-tolerant prescription
\widetilde{FT}(\cdot)
corresponding to inner simulation. Notice that the exRecs for the toric code gadgets overlap not only in time but also in space, specifically on
k
-links. Thus, in addition to dealing with spatial truncation, we will also need to utilize a modified procedure for dealing with temporal truncation. Temporal truncation as well as the prescription for “pulling” the
\ast
-decoder through the layers of simulation will be explained in Subsec.
5.2.3
.

We first introduce the concept of spatial truncation:

Definition 5.5

(Spatial truncation)
.

A spatially truncated exRec for gadget
\mathcal{G}_{k}
is one where one or more
k
-links or sites in
\Sigma_{k}
are removed from the exRec’s spatial support throughout the temporal duration of the exRec.

Denoting by
\Lambda
the spatial region that is removed and the set of times corresponding to the operation of the exRec by
\tau
, the truncation region of an exRec is a spacetime region of removed links and sites
\Lambda\times\tau
.

Definition 5.6

(Good/bad truncated exRecs)
.

Consider a spatially truncated exRec
R
centered around gadget
\mathcal{G}_{k}
. Count the faults belonging to this exRec in the following way. For each failure of an elementary gadget
\mathcal{G}_{0}^{(i)}
fully contained in
\widetilde{EC}^{\otimes K}\circ\mathcal{G}_{k}\circ\widetilde{EC}^{\otimes K}
, consider the restriction of the associated operator
\mathcal{O}
applied by these gadgets (including noise) to the truncated boundary
\lambda
with the bad exRec that is causing truncation. If
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{O})\subseteq\lambda
, we formally count the fault of this gadget towards the neighboring bad exRec that caused the truncation. Otherwise, we count this fault towards the truncated exRec under consideration.

We will use the following algorithm for exRec assignment:

Algorithm 1

(Assigning support to exRecs)
.

Assume a fixed level
k
for the inner simulation and let the associated outer simulation applied to circuit
\mathcal{C}
yield the simulated circuit
\widetilde{FT}(\mathcal{C})
. Call
T
the depth of
\mathcal{C}
. We use the time index
t
to enumerate the layers of the simulated level-
k
gates
\mathcal{G}_{k}
associated to the gates in
\mathcal{C}
. Then:

1.

Construct exRecs associated with gates
\mathcal{G}_{k}
at a specific time slice
t\leq T
.

2.

Pick an arbitrary order to traverse the exRecs in space at this time slice. If a given exRec is bad, spatially truncate all surrounding exRecs at the same time slice which have overlapping spatial support with the given bad exRec.

This assigns support and good and bad labels for the last layer of exRecs in a given simulated circuit (illustrated in Figure
5
).

As we will see shortly, in order to pull layers of the hierarchical ideal decoder (Def.
3.27
) through a simulated circuit, we first use the algorithm above to assign supports of exRecs and mark them as good and bad and then pull the ideal decoder through this layer. We then repeat this process layer by layer.

To explain this in more depth, we need to introduce the
*
-decoder, which will work analogously to its operation in the usual exRecs method (see Def.
B.6
and discussion afterwards), except that now it is applied to an entire
layer
of exRecs at once:

Figure 5
:
Schematic showing an outer simulation for the toric code automaton and the outcome of one application of Algorithm
1
. The truncation region is schematically shaded dark.
In this figure and in the rest of the paper, the crossed wires are used to schematically denote multiple qubits in the support of the operations.

Definition 5.7

(
*
-decoder for the toric code)
.

Assume a fixed level
k
for the inner simulation and the associated outer simulation prescription that we use to obtain a simulated circuit
\widetilde{FT}(\mathcal{C})
and run Algorithm
1
to label good and bad exRecs and perform truncation. We append a
*
-decoder
\widetilde{D^{*}}
at the end of the circuit and define its operation to be:

(a)

For the layer of exRecs that directly precedes the decoder, the decoder stores the syndromes of the noise operators applied in their spacetime support in its classical register, and

(b)

applies an ideal decoder
\widetilde{D}
.

The output of the
\ast
-decoder is the output of the ideal decoder
\widetilde{D}
together with a classical register containing the data in (a). Partial tracing out the classical register, this defines a quantum channel supported on qubits of the outputs of bad exRecs.

Similarly to the ideal decoder, the
\ast
-decoder is not an actual physical operation but rather a mathematical tool that we will use to ‘undo’ levels of simulation in the toric code automaton. Schematically, the
\ast
-decoder’s operation can be summarized as follows:

where the block on the left stands for the layer of exRecs, and the blue outgoing wire stands for the register with classical information. On the right, the operation of the star decoder can be written as an ideal decoder that consists of applying a noiseless layer of
\widetilde{EC}
followed by a
k
-fold pushforward map
\Phi^{k}
(which is just
k
applications of
\Phi
), along with the relevant classical register.

We will also need to define a different notion of a filter than the one used for concatenated codes.
Recall that the role of a filter is to project onto a subspace of states that are not too “far” from a codestate at the corresponding level of the simulation. A natural way to define this for the toric code is to compare the state with a level-
k
coarse-grained version obtained by applying
\mathcal{L}(EC_{k})
. Thus, for each
k
-cell, we define an
m
-cell filter (with
m\leq k-1
) to project on the subspace of states that differ from their coarse-grained version by an operator (call it
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
) whose support fits in an
m
-cell. We also define a
\varnothing
-filter as the filter that forbids the state to differ from a level-
k
coarse-grained state.

However, the definition that we present below is somewhat more involved than this description to account for the fact that neighboring
k
-cells share boundaries. In this case, we first need to specify which filters are assigned to all neighboring cells (if any). Considering boundaries of a specific
k
-cell
\mathcal{C}_{k}
with an
m
-cell filter, there are several scenarios. For a given boundary, if there is no filter on the other
k
-cell that shares this boundary, then we allow anything to happen on the boundary. If there is another
m
-cell filter or
\varnothing
-filter on the other
k
-cell, the
m
-cell within
\mathcal{C}_{k}
containing
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
is allowed to touch the shared boundary. This applies to every
k
-cell with a filter.
All these scenarios are summarized in the definition below and in Fig.
6
.

Figure 6
:

A pair of
k
-cells
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{(i)}
and
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{(i+1)}
with various types of cell filters. The type of the cell filter imposed on
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{(i)}
and
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{(i+1)}
cell is indicated at the top.
All other
k
-cells (i.e. outside of the ones depicted) have no filter assigned to them.
The check marks indicate that the state passes the appropriate filter, and the cross marks indicate that it does not.
For the examples that pass the filter configuration, for each cell with an
m
-cell filter, there exists a relative damage operator
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
at level
k
(i.e. an operator relating the state to its level-
k
coarse-grained version) that fits inside an
m
-cell, shaded in red.
For the examples that do not pass the filter configuration,
any
relative damage operator violates the filter conditions. The red boxes show
m
-cells containing the support of a relative damage operator.

Definition 5.8

(
m
-cell filter at level
k
for the toric code)
.

We say that a layer of filters has been added or assigned to a toric code state
\ket{\psi}
with syndrome
\sigma
if each
k
-cell with
k>m
17
17

17

Unless otherwise specified,
k
coincides with the level of inner simulation for the toric code automaton.
has been assigned an
m
-cell filter,
\varnothing
-cell filter, or no filter. Each filter associated with a given
k
-cell
\mathcal{C}_{k}
is a projector onto the subspace of states that satisfy the conditions below. We will say a state “passes” the filter if it is in the image of the associated projector.

Suppose the state
\ket{\psi}
is related to the initial logical state
\ket{\phi}
of the toric code by
\ket{\psi}=\mathcal{O}\ket{\phi}
, with syndrome configuration
\sigma
. Assuming a filter assignment, we obtain a level-
k
coarse-grained syndrome configuration by applying a noiseless error correction layer
\mathcal{L}(EC_{k})[\sigma]
. We denote by
\mathcal{O}_{cg}
the product of all feedback operators applied by
\mathcal{L}(EC_{k})
. Define an equivalence class of operators
[\mathcal{O}_{cg}]=\{\mathcal{O}:\mathcal{O}\mathcal{O}_{cg}\in\mathcal{S}\}
.
For the filter conditions under a given filter assignment to be met, we require that there exists an operator
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\in[\mathcal{O}_{cg}]
with qubit support
M
, such that the following is satisfied.

For each cell
\mathcal{C}_{k}
that has an
m
-cell filter:

•

M\cap(\mathcal{C}_{k}\setminus\partial\mathcal{C}_{k})
fits within an
m
-cell. Here, supports are taken to be on qubits rather than vertices.

•

For every boundary
\Lambda
between
\mathcal{C}_{k}
and a neighboring cell
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\prime}
:

(i)

If the neighboring cell
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\prime}
has an
m
-cell filter, then there exist
m
-cells
\mathcal{C}_{m}\in\mathcal{C}_{k}
and
\mathcal{C}_{m}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\prime}
such that:

M\cap\Lambda=((M\cap\mathcal{C}_{m})\cup(M\cap\mathcal{C}_{m}^{\prime}))\cap\Lambda.

(5.12)

(ii)

If the neighboring
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\prime}
has a
\varnothing
-cell filter, then
there exists an
m
-cell
\mathcal{C}_{m}\in\mathcal{C}_{k}
such that:

M\cap\Lambda=(M\cap\mathcal{C}_{m})\cap\Lambda.

(5.13)

•

If there is no filter on the neighboring cell
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\prime}
, no condition is imposed on the boundary
\Lambda
.

For each cell
\mathcal{C}_{k}
that has a
\varnothing
-cell filter:

•

M\cap(\mathcal{C}_{k}\setminus\partial\mathcal{C}_{k})=\varnothing
.

•

For every boundary
\Lambda
between
\mathcal{C}_{k}
and a neighboring cell
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\prime}
:

(i)

If the neighboring cell
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\prime}
has an
m
-cell filter, then there exists an
m
-cell
\mathcal{C}_{m}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\prime}
such that:

M\cap\Lambda=(M\cap\mathcal{C}_{m}^{\prime})\cap\Lambda.

(5.14)

(ii)

If the neighboring
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\prime}
has a
\varnothing
-cell filter, then

M\cap\Lambda=\varnothing.

(5.15)

(iii)

If there is no filter on
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\prime}
, no condition is imposed on
\Lambda
.

We will use a convention where
m=0
will stand for
\varnothing
-filter, whereas
m\geq 1
will stand for an
m
-cell filter. The operator
\mathcal{O}_{cg}
in the definition above relates the syndrome configuration of the state
\ket{\psi}
to the level-
k
coarse-grained version of this syndrome configuration.

Thus, in the presence of a layer of filters, all
k
-cells can be grouped into a set of contiguous clusters where each
k
-cell in a cluster is covered by either an
m
-cell or
\varnothing
-cell filter, and
k
-cells not in any cluster do not have a filter assignment. The conditions in Def. (
5.8
) are imposed on the syndrome configuration in the interior of each contiguous cluster minus its boundary, since no constraint is imposed on boundaries of clusters or
k
-cells not in any cluster.
A cluster formed by two
k
-cells
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{(i)}
and
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{(i+1)}
is shown in Fig.
6
along with examples of damage configurations that either pass or do not pass a pair of adjacent filters of a given type.

Moving forward, when a state
\ket{\psi}=\mathcal{O}\ket{\phi}
from the definition above passes an
m
-cell filter, we will also say that the damage
\mathcal{O}
passes the
m
-cell filter. We will refer to the operator
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
from the definition above as relative damage at level
k
. When we say that the damage has been “removed” or “cleaned up” at level
k
, we mean that a correction operation
\mathcal{O}^{\prime\prime}
has been applied by the automaton so that
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\mathcal{O}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
.

We can now define the EC and gate properties for the toric code automaton:

Definition 5.9

(
(\mathrm{Gate})_{k,m}
and
(EC)_{k,m}
conditions for toric code)
.

Consider a layer of level-
k
gadgets. For the
(\mathrm{Gate}\ A)_{k,m}
conditions, as well as the
(\mathrm{Gate}\ B)_{k,m}
and
(\mathrm{EC}\ B)_{k,m}
conditions, we assume a layer with
\varnothing
or
m
-cell filters present on each
k
-cell in the input state (see Def.
5.8
).

The
(\mathrm{Gate}\ A)_{k,m}
and
(\mathrm{EC}\ A)_{k,m}
conditions append a layer of cell filters after the layer of level
k
gadgets via

where we schematically show the input and output qubits as doubled lines (which, in reality, include qubits shared between the supports of neighboring
k
-cells).
s^{(i)}
denotes the number of failures of elementary gadgets within the corresponding level-
k
gadget and
m_{j}^{(i)}\in\{0,m\}
determines the size of the cell filter imposed on the
j
-th
k
-cell in the support of
i
-th gadget. In addition, we require that
\sum_{j=1}^{K^{(i)}}\frac{m^{(i)}_{j}}{m}+s^{(i)}\leq 1
for each gadget in
(\mathrm{Gate}\ A)_{k,m}
and
s^{(i)}\leq 1
in
(\mathrm{EC}\ A)_{k,m}
.
If this holds, a
\left(\sum_{j=1}^{K^{(i)}}m^{(i)}_{j}+s^{(i)}m\right)
-cell filter is assigned to the output cells of each gate
\mathcal{G}_{k}^{(i)}
.
For the
(\mathrm{EC}\ A)_{k,m}
condition, a
(s^{(i)}m)
-cell filter is assigned to the output
k
-cell for each
\widetilde{EC}
gadget.

For the
(\mathrm{Gate}\ B)_{k,m}
and
(\mathrm{EC}\ B)_{k,m}
conditions, each gate or
\widetilde{EC}
gadget in the layer satisfies the following conditions:

where the triangle represents the ideal decoder.
For the
(\mathrm{Gate}\ B)_{k,m}
, we assume that the condition
\sum_{j=1}^{K^{(i)}}\frac{m_{j}^{(i)}}{m}+s^{(i)}\leq 1
is fulfilled for each gate gadget. For the
(\mathrm{EC}\ B)_{k,m}
condition, we similarly assume that
\frac{m^{(i)}}{m}+s^{(i)}\leq 1
is fulfilled for each gadget.

The A-type condition is imposed on a whole layer of gadgets at once because all cell filters are determined simultaneously (since information about which filter to place depends on neighboring filters). In addition, because the ideal decoder is
global
, its action cannot be separated into disjoint blocks, and, as a consequence, the B-type condition is also imposed on the entire layer.

Note also that for the
EC
B and Gate B conditions (we will largely omit the subscript
k,m
for convenience) one pulls the entire layer of gadgets through the ideal decoder as opposed to pulling one codeblock at a time like for concatenated codes
18
18

18

An alternative definition of
EC
B and Gate B condition where one pulls the action of separate gadgets through the ideal decoder (which would look much more similar to the one in the original exRecs method) could also work. However, it requires a set of careful definitions because the supports of gadgets overlap. As we will shortly see, the layer-wise definition of these conditions adopted here is sufficient and provides a simpler framework for proving fault tolerance.
.

5.2.2
Confinement in the operator language

For the proof of our main theorem, we will need to translate ideas like confinement to the operator perspective. In this subsection, we discuss what the confinement property implies for operators that are applied due to the action of the gadget.

Recall that an elementary gadget acting at time
t
applies local Pauli feedback
F_{t}
conditioned on the syndromes of the input state in its support, followed by the application of the Pauli noise operator
P_{t}
restricted to its support. The total operator applied as a consequence of the gadget action is their product
P_{t}F_{t}
. In the toric code automaton, elementary gadgets are defined such that a whole layer of them at time
t
can operate simultaneously. As a consequence, to determine the result of their operation, we can consider each of them independently and then add their outputs. This cannot be done for composite gadgets because they share boundaries. As a result, we can still define the operator applied due to the action of a composite gadget but now we have to consider an entire layer of gadgets at once:

Definition 5.10

(Operator applied by composite gadget)
.

Consider a layer of gadgets, denoted
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G})
. Define
t=t_{0}
to be the start of the layer of gadgets and
t=t_{1}
to be the end. For a noise realization
\bm{H}
with operator history
\vec{P}=\{P_{1},...P_{T}\}
(see Def.
A.10
) and an input state with syndrome
\sigma
, call
\{F_{t}(\sigma_{t})\}_{t=t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
to be the Pauli feedback operators applied by the layer of gadget at each timestep (which depend on the gadgets, input syndrome and the noise).

Define the operator
\mathcal{O}[\sigma,\vec{P};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G})]
to be
\prod_{t=t_{0}}^{t_{1}}P_{t}F_{t}(\sigma_{t})
, which is the cumulative operator applied by the operator history and the gadget feedback.
We will often omit the argument
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G})
since it can usually be deduced from the context.

We will also denote
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{R}}[\sigma,\vec{P};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G})]
to be the restriction of the operator
\mathcal{O}[\sigma,\vec{P};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G})]
to the qubits in the region
\mathcal{R}
. In particular, we can write it as
\prod_{t=t_{0}}^{t_{1}}P_{t}\big|_{\mathcal{R}}F_{t}(\sigma_{t})\big|_{\mathcal{R}}
.

Recall that, unless otherwise specified, we assume all operators applied due to noise and feedback are of the Pauli-
X
type.

Definition 5.11

(Restriction of operator arguments)
.

Consider an input state with syndromes
\sigma
and noise realization
\bm{H}
with noise operator history
\vec{P}=\{P_{1},\cdots,P_{T}\}
. Consider also a spatial region
\mathcal{Q}
. Define
\mathcal{O}\left[\sigma\big|_{\mathcal{Q}},\vec{P}\big|_{\mathcal{Q}};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G})\right]
to be the operator applied by the layer of gadgets
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G})
assuming that input syndromes are restricted to the vertices in
\mathcal{Q}
and the noise is restricted to the qubits in
\mathcal{Q}
. The notation
\vec{P}\big|_{\mathcal{Q}}
stands for
\{P_{1}\big|_{\mathcal{Q}},\cdots,P_{T}\big|_{\mathcal{Q}}\}
.

Note that restricting operator arguments to a given region is not the same as performing the restriction at the end.

Lemma 5.1

(Operator confinement part 1)
.

Consider a layer of level-
k
gadgets
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})
of the toric code automaton acting on an input state with syndromes
\sigma
along with a noise realization
\bm{H}
with operator history
\vec{P}
. Call
\mathcal{R}_{j}
the qubit support of a gadget
\mathcal{G}_{k}^{(j)}
. Fixing a particular gadget
\mathcal{G}_{k}^{(i)}
, let
\Lambda
be the set of
k
-links in the boundary of
\mathcal{R}_{i}
, define the extended operator support
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
of
\mathcal{G}_{k}^{(i)}
to be

\mathcal{Q}_{i}=\mathcal{R}_{i}\cup\left(\bigcup_{j:\,\mathcal{R}_{j}\cap\mathcal{R}_{i}\subseteq\Lambda}\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{j}\right).

(5.16)

where we introduce the notation
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{j}=\mathcal{R}_{j}\setminus\partial\mathcal{R}_{j}
. Then

\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}[\sigma,\vec{P};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})]=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}[\sigma\big|_{\mathcal{Q}_{i}},\vec{P}\big|_{\mathcal{Q}_{i}};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})].

(5.17)

Proof.

The proof is split into two steps. First, we show that the operator
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}}[\sigma,\vec{P};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})]
only depends on syndromes and noise realization restricted to
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
. Then we show that the operator
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda_{ij}}[\sigma,\vec{P};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})]
supported on the boundary
\lambda_{ij}
(which is a set of
k
-links) between
\mathcal{R}_{i}
and
\mathcal{R}_{j}
only depends on syndromes and the noise in the region
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}\cup\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{j}\cup\lambda_{ij}
. The Lemma then immediately follows because

\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}=\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}}[\sigma,\vec{P};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})]\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{j}\mathcal{O}_{\lambda_{ij}}[\sigma,\vec{P};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})]\right).

To show the first step, we write
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}}[\sigma,\vec{P};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})]=\prod_{t=t_{0}}^{t_{1}}P_{t}\big|_{\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}}F_{t}\big|_{\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}}
. We first argue that the feedback operators in the qubit support of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
only depend on syndromes in this region. Any level-0 constituent gadget which is a part of higher-level gadget of the toric code automaton either (a) is conditioned on vertices in region
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
and applies feedback with qubit support in this region or (b) is conditioned on vertices in one of the boundary
\lambda_{ij}
and applies feedback with qubit support on
\lambda_{ij}
(an induction-based proof of this is essentially given in the proof of Proposition
5.1
). Restricting to
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
, we see that the total feedback operator must be conditioned on syndromes supported in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
. The syndromes in this region at each time step will depend on the input syndromes in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
and the syndromes of the noise operators
P_{t}
for
t=[t_{0},t_{1}]
restricted to
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
, which we denote
\varepsilon_{\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}}
. Because
\varepsilon_{\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}}
can be uniquely determined by restricting the operator itself to
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
(i.e.
P_{t}\big|_{\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}}
), we have

\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}}[\sigma,\vec{P};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})]=\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}}[\sigma\big|_{\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}},\vec{P}\big|_{\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})].

(5.18)

Next, we argue that
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda_{ij}}[\sigma,\vec{P};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})]
depends only on syndromes in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}\cup\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{j}\cup\lambda_{ij}
. First, we note that endpoints of
\lambda_{ij}
are linear vertices of all gadgets of level
s\leq k
by Proposition
5.1
, and thus the operator that is applied along the link does not depend on syndromes at these points. Using Proposition
5.1
, we note that the region
\partial\mathcal{R}_{i}\cup\partial\mathcal{R}_{j}\setminus\lambda_{ij}
is a confinement region. Therefore, using Fact
3
for this region as well as the observation that the level-0 gadgets that apply feedback supported on qubits in
\lambda_{ij}
are only conditioned on syndromes in
\lambda_{ij}
, we write

\mathcal{O}_{\lambda_{ij}}[\sigma,\vec{P};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})]=\mathcal{O}_{\lambda_{ij}}[\sigma\big|_{\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}\cup\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{j}\cup\lambda_{ij}},\vec{P}\big|_{\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}\cup\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{j}\cup\lambda_{ij}};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})].

(5.19)

Finally, using that the union of the regions
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{i}\cup\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{j}\cup\lambda_{ij}
for all
j
(i.e. corresponding to all gadgets
\mathcal{G}_{k}^{(j)}
neighboring
\mathcal{G}_{k}^{(i)}
) is
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
, we obtain the statement of the Lemma.
∎

Lemma 5.2

(Operator confinement part 2)
.

Under the assumptions of the previous Lemma, consider instead the truncated support of the gadget
\mathcal{G}^{(i)}_{k}
defined as
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{i}=\mathcal{R}_{i}\setminus\widetilde{\Lambda}
where
\widetilde{\Lambda}=\{\lambda_{j}\}
is the subset of the set of four boundaries that are truncated. Then, the following holds:

\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{i}}[\sigma,\vec{P};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})]=\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{i}}[\sigma\big|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}},\vec{P}\big|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}};\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})]

(5.20)

where the “truncated” extended operator support is defined as

\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}=\mathcal{Q}_{i}\setminus\bigcup_{j:\,\mathcal{R}_{i}\cap\mathcal{R}_{j}\subseteq\widetilde{\Lambda}}\mathcal{R}_{j}.

(5.21)

Namely, we remove the supports of gadgets that neighbor with
\mathcal{G}^{(i)}_{k}
over the truncated boundaries.

Proof.

First decompose

\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{i}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{R}_{i}\setminus\partial\mathcal{R}_{i}}\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{j:\lambda_{ij}\notin\widetilde{\Lambda}}\mathcal{O}_{\lambda_{ij}}\right)

and apply considerations from the proof of previous Lemma to each of the operators in the tensor product.
∎

5.2.3
Proof of the modified exRecs method

In this subsection, we will finally prove that, given a circuit
\mathcal{C}
for the
X
-type automaton, one iteration of
X
-type outer fault tolerant simulation
\widetilde{FT}(\mathcal{C})
which experiences a
p
-bounded decoupled gadget error model will result in an
Ap^{2}
-bounded decoupled gadget error model for
\mathcal{C}
. This will be shown assuming that the automaton gadgets satisfy the Gate and EC conditions from Def.
5.9
for some
k,m
. The proof will require us to heavily use the operator language rather than the syndrome language, and the results of the previous subsection will be useful. In general, iterating this fault tolerant simulation will ensure exponential logical error suppression and a threshold. In the next subsection, we will prove that the particular gadgets that we use satisfy the gate and EC properties, and after combining the
X
and
Z
sectors of error correction together, will then prove a threshold for the full quantum automaton.

Proposition 5.2
.

Consider an inner simulation and an associated outer simulation
\widetilde{FT}(\cdot)
. Assume that the gate and error correction gadgets satisfy
(\mathrm{Gate})_{k,m}
and
(EC)_{k,m}
for some
k
corresponding to the inner simulation level and some
m
.

Consider a layer of level-
k
gates along with leading and trailing layers of the level-
k
EC gadget, which we will denote
\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{EC})\circ\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})\circ\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{EC})
. Follow this layer by an ideal hierarchical level-
k
decoder which we denote
\widetilde{D}
. If all exRecs covering
\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{EC})\circ\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})\circ\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{EC})
are good, then

\widetilde{D}\circ\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{EC})\circ\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})\circ\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{EC})=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{0})\circ\widetilde{D}\circ\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{EC})

(5.22)

where each level-0 gate gadget in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{0})
on the right hand side is the level-reduced and noiseless version of the associated level-
k
gate gadget.

Proof.

As reviewed in Prop.
B.1
, the proof from
[
26
]
can be adapted almost verbatim to prove this proposition: specifically,
EC
and Gate conditions are applied in the same way as in the proof of Prop.
B.1
. The only difference is that an entire layer of gadgets is pulled through the ideal decoder simultaneously whenever
EC
B or Gate B conditions are applied.
∎

Before proving a version of the above result that allows for some bad exRecs to be present, we need an additional definition that will suitably modify the operation of a layer of gadgets whenever the layer intersects with the support of bad exRecs. This procedure replaces the usual truncation procedure in the conventional exRecs formalism, which is discussed in App.
B
.

Definition 5.12

(Temporal truncation)
.

Assume a fault-tolerant simulation of circuit
\mathcal{C}
of elementary gates using the inner and outer simulation prescription of Secs.
3.5.2
and
3.5.3
.

Consider a layer of exRecs corresponding to
\mathcal{L}_{tr}(\widetilde{EC})\circ\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})\circ\mathcal{L}_{le}(\widetilde{EC})
, where we label the leading and trailing layers of error correction gadgets as ‘
le
’ and ‘
tr
’, respectively. Assume that the
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{k})
,
\mathcal{L}_{tr}(\widetilde{EC})
, and the succeeding layer of level-
k
gates (not contained in the layer of exRecs under consideration) are start at times
t_{0}
,
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
, see Fig.
7
. The following procedure proceeds backward in time.

Suppose that
t_{0}
